Inc., 1992 1 \n    33→--- PAGE 4 ---\n    34→IN I - TECHNOLOGY ~ \n    35→HOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \n    36→The process of producing high quality solder joints is \n    37→similar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \n    38→If you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \n    39→impossible. However, once you understand and imple- \n    40→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    41→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    42→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    43→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    44→below: \n    45→SURFACES MUST BE \n    46→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    47→II MUST BE \n    48→11 SMALL \n    49→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    50→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    51→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    52→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    53→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    54→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n    55→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n    56→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n    57→MATERIALS \n    58→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n    59→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n    60→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n    61→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n    62→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n    63→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n    64→solderability. MACHINES \n    65→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n    66→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n    67→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n    68→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n    69→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n    70→able. \n    71→MANPOWER \n    72→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n    73→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n    74→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n    75→operating it. \n    76→METHODS \n    77→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n    78→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n    79→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n    80→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n    81→including all data that pertains to it. \n    82→ENVIRONMENT \n    83→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n    84→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n    85→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n    86→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n    87→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n    88→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n    89→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n    90→again may cause a defect. \n    91→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n    92→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n    93→Producing high quality solder joints \n    94→requires that five general process \n    95→variables need to be in control; the \n    96→materials, machines, manpower, \n    97→methods, and the environment. \n    98→2 Section I - Technology \n    99→--- PAGE 5 ---\n   100→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   101→I I I I I I 1; I :I I I I I In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   102→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   103→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   104→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   105→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   106→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   107→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   108→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   109→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   110→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   111→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   112→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   113→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   114→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   115→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   116→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   117→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   118→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   119→there is considerable truth to what was said. I \n   120→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   121→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   122→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   123→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   124→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   125→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   126→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   127→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   128→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   129→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   130→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   131→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   132→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   133→must first be defined and then maintained. 280- \n   134→260- \n   135→240- \n   136→T 220- \n   137→High quality, low defect soldering \n   138→requires identifying the optimum \n   139→temperature profile for reflowing \n   140→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   141→over and over. I WETTING ; ‘ TIME , \n   142→30-60 .’ : SEC ~ \n   143→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   144→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   145→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   146→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   147→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   148→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   149→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   150→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   151→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   152→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   153→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   154→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   155→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   156→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   157→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   158→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   159→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   160→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   161→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   162→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   163→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   164→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   165→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   166→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   167→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   168→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   169→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   170→Section I - Technology 3 \n   171→--- PAGE 6 ---\n   172→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   173→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   174→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   175→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   176→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   177→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   178→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   179→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   180→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   181→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   182→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   183→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   184→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   185→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   186→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   187→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   188→three zones. reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   189→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   190→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   191→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   192→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   193→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   194→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   195→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   196→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   197→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   198→set at the same value. \n   199→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   200→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   201→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   202→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   203→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   204→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   205→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   206→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   207→200 c \n   208→I PREHEAT1 I PREHEAT2 I REFLOW I \n   209→I \n   210→I \n   211→I \n   212→I \n   213→I \n   214→I \n   215→I \n   216→I \n   217→I \n   218→I \n   219→COOLING I \n   220→TIME \n   221→4 Section I - Technology \n   222→--- PAGE 7 ---\n   223→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   224→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   225→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   226→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   227→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   228→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   229→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   230→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   231→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   232→SOLDER SPATTER \n   233→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   234→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   235→FICIENT DRYING. BURNT BOARDS, \n   236→DEWET SOLDER fi COLD SOLDER \n   237→JOINTS, DULL \n   238→SOLDER JOINTS \n   239→(TOO COLD) \n   240→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   241→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   242→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   243→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   244→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   245→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   246→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   247→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   248→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   249→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   250→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   251→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   252→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   253→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   254→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   255→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   256→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   257→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   258→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   259→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   260→DEFINE THE BAND \n   261→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   262→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   263→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   264→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   265→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   266→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   267→results. \n   268→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   269→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   270→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   271→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   272→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   273→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   274→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   275→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   276→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   277→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   278→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   279→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   280→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   281→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   282→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   283→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   284→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   285→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   286→must be exposed. \n   287→The total temperature deviation from \n   288→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   289→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   290→the band. \n   291→Section I - Technology 5 \n   292→--- PAGE 8 ---\n   293→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   294→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   295→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   296→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   297→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   298→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   299→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   300→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   301→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   302→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   303→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   304→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   305→AT = Q x AX t / (M x Cp) \n   306→WHERE: \n   307→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   308→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   309→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   310→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   311→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   312→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   313→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   314→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   315→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   316→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   317→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   318→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   319→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   320→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   321→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   322→profile. \n   323→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL J \n   324→mmmm \n   325→mmmm \n   326→mmmm \n   327→mmmm \n   328→mmmm \n   329→mmmm OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   330→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   331→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   332→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   333→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   334→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   335→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   336→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   337→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   338→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   339→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   340→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   341→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   342→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   343→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   344→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   345→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   346→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   347→the figure. \n   348→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN b \n   349→L \n   350→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   351→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   352→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   353→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   354→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   355→important consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \n   356→uniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \n   357→running a profile on a test board, then load the oven \n   358→down. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   359→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   360→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   361→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   362→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   363→and product loading. \n   364→6 Section I - Technology \n   365→--- PAGE 9 ---\n   366→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   367→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   368→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   369→and infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \n   370→modes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \n   371→easiest way to understand each of these heating mode \n   372→is through example. \n   373→CONVECTION \n   374→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \n   375→air, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \n   376→SMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \n   377→provides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \n   378→provides convective heating. Convection heating c \n   379→cooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \n   380→Only the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \n   381→is actually transferring heat. \n   382→2ONVECTION I - \n   383→HOT . \n   384→AIR \\ \n   385→FLOW - COLD OBJECT IS - HEATED BY CONVECTION \n   386→FROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \n   387→CONDUCTION \n   388→Conduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \n   389→of different temperature are in contact with each other \n   390→A good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \n   391→burner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \n   392→contact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \n   393→occurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \n   394→tial exists within the mass. \n   395→Referring to a system as 100% convection or \n   396→100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \n   397→by convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \n   398→always present in signijicant quantities. CONDUCTION \n   399→HEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \n   400→EQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \n   401→RADIATION \n   402→Infrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \n   403→ferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \n   404→example of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \n   405→is a non-contact heat transfer. \n   406→RADIATION I \n   407→In SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \n   408→phase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \n   409→the heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \n   410→convection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \n   411→dominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \n   412→fer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \n   413→in the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \n   414→dominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n   415→80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \n   416→product during the heating process. Heat is transferred \n   417→through the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \n   418→Section I - Technology 7 \n   419→--- PAGE 10 ---\n   420→HOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n   421→95.38 Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \n   422→different temperatures are in sight of each other. The \n   423→heat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n   424→1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \n   425→of infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \n   426→the wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \n   427→the absolute temperature of the object. As the source \n   428→temperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \n   429→exponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \n   430→temperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \n   431→the source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \n   432→SMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \n   433→as not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \n   434→these low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n   435→(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \n   436→wavelength ranges. \n   437→111111lI INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \n   438→In order to understand what parameters are important in \n   439→infrared heating, one can consider the general equation \n   440→for heat transfer between the heat source and the object \n   441→being heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \n   442→equation, but rather to point out the significance of what \n   443→is involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \n   444→follows: \n   445→Q = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \n   446→WHERE: \n   447→Q = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \n   448→V = Geometric view factor (0-1) \n   449→Es = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \n   450→At = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \n   451→K = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \n   452→Ts = Source temperature (OK) \n   453→Tt = Target temperature (OK) (5.67 x w/cm2/”K4) \n   454→Absorption characteristics of solder \n   455→paste and circuit board material makes \n   456→IR an efficient means of heating. The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \n   457→that leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \n   458→the oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \n   459→in the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \n   460→view factor comes in product design. If two very large \n   461→components are in close proximity to each other, the \n   462→view factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \n   463→which makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \n   464→f IRSOURCE \n   465→THIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \n   466→ADJACENT COMPONENTS \n   467→RESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \n   468→FACTOR \n   469→The source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \n   470→are in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \n   471→tions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \n   472→energy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \n   473→as they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \n   474→board material, solder paste, and the components all \n   475→absorb quite well. The figure below shows the spectral \n   476→absorptivity for an RMA solder paste.(3) \n   477→I \n   478→loooo t \n   479→! 97.39 I4 I \n   480→96’70 96.04 I! \n   481→Control of infrared is generally done by controlling the \n   482→source temperature. Providing that the overall source \n   483→emission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \n   484→repeatability. \n   485→8 Section I - Technology \n   486→--- PAGE 11 ---\n   487→HOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \n   488→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \n   489→temperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \n   490→ture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \n   491→heated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \n   492→be cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \n   493→tion is to look at the convection equation and note what \n   494→is important and what is not important. \n   495→CONVECTION EQUATION \n   496→The following is the general equation for convection \n   497→heating or cooling: \n   498→Q=HxAT \n   499→WHERE: \n   500→Q = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \n   501→H = Convective film coefficient \n   502→AT = Temperature difference between (W/cm2-”C) \n   503→fluid and object (“C) \n   504→The amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \n   505→either changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \n   506→temperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \n   507→temperature will increase the temperature difference, \n   508→and thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \n   509→proportionally. \n   510→The convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \n   511→impact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \n   512→related to a number of variables, including velocity of \n   513→the flow and angle of attack of the flow. \n   514→Increasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n   515→“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \n   516→low velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \n   517→nice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n   518→~ ~~ ~~ \n   519→Perpendicular flow convection breaks \n   520→up surface thermal boundary layers, \n   521→which enhances heat transfer. very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \n   522→a higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \n   523→more effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \n   524→cations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \n   525→the amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \n   526→tical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \n   527→components to shift around. The flow direction also has \n   528→a significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \n   529→is shown below: \n   530→PARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \n   531→I FLOW DIRECTION 4 - \n   532→BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   533→CAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \n   534→BOARD, WHICH REDUCES \n   535→HEAT TRANSFER \n   536→Note that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \n   537→referred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \n   538→layer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \n   539→heat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \n   540→the boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \n   541→freely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \n   542→perpendicular flow convection: \n   543→PERPENDICULAR FLOW \n   544→FLOW DIRECTION \n   545→ttt \n   546→NO BOUNDARY \n   547→LAYER \n   548→NO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   549→ENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \n   550→TRANSFER \n   551→In the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \n   552→there is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \n   553→perpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \n   554→cooling results. \n   555→Section I - Technology 9 \n   556→--- PAGE 12 ---\n   557→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \n   558→In SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \n   559→pure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n   560→100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \n   561→over the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \n   562→in not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \n   563→would need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \n   564→the case. However, some systems are more dominated by \n   565→convection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \n   566→source. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \n   567→convection dominated machine as a convection ma- \n   568→chine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \n   569→CONVECTION \n   570→Both convection and IR have their unique advantages \n   571→and limitations. In convective systems, typically the \n   572→temperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \n   573→ture of the product in a given zone. This has the \n   574→advantage in that the product temperature will never \n   575→exceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \n   576→heating a product that has a large difference in the mass \n   577→of the components and distribution. In this case, the \n   578→larger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \n   579→components will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \n   580→is not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \n   581→heat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \n   582→may be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \n   583→efficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \n   584→CONVECTION \n   585→Less sensitive to shading \n   586→Heats more uniformly \n   587→AT (Source-target) is small \n   588→Low thermal mass of air requires \n   589→high flow rates to transfer heat \n   590→Inefficient (20 to 30%) \n   591→Will heat leads first, increasing \n   592→chance of solder wicking. is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \n   593→loop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \n   594→reheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \n   595→limitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \n   596→levels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \n   597→that if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \n   598→soldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \n   599→product becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \n   600→ucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \n   601→as typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \n   602→IR \n   603→IR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \n   604→efficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \n   605→solder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \n   606→have the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \n   607→needed. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \n   608→dense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \n   609→limitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \n   610→between the source and the product is required to transfer \n   611→the heat. Products with high component mass differen- \n   612→tial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \n   613→to accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \n   614→reflowing products with component sizes up to and \n   615→including 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \n   616→arrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \n   617→system. \n   618→IR \n   619→Control source temperature only \n   620→High efficiency (60-70%) \n   621→Easy to control rate of rise \n   622→More sensitive to shading \n   623→Less uniform high mass differences \n   624→AT (Source-target) is large \n   625→10 Section I - Technology \n   626→--- PAGE 13 ---\n   627→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n   628→~ \n   629→Ease of cleaning flux \n   630→residues (RMA) Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \n   631→a significant process consideration. The issue is really \n   632→not the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \n   633→to reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \n   634→the presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \n   635→generally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \n   636→soldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \n   637→fluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \n   638→future trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \n   639→paste. The future trends include two major process \n   640→changes that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \n   641→atmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n   642→500 ppm FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \n   643→Fine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \n   644→to obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \n   645→sizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \n   646→particles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \n   647→volume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \n   648→RATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \n   649→RATIO = 6 / D \n   650→WHERE: \n   651→D = Particle Diameter \n   652→FOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \n   653→GREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \n   654→WITH LARGER PARTICLES. \n   655→Thus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \n   656→With more available surface area in the solder paste, \n   657→there is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \n   658→mind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \n   659→the temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \n   660→and the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \n   661→that one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \n   662→This can only be done through proper oven design. NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \n   663→The second technology change that is driving the need \n   664→towards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \n   665→clean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \n   666→is creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \n   667→requires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \n   668→nents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \n   669→process must not add any significant oxidation to the \n   670→process. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \n   671→aggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \n   672→cleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \n   673→product. These solder balls eventually can create short \n   674→circuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \n   675→in a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \n   676→bility of creating solder balls. \n   677→A good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \n   678→ment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \n   679→National Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \n   680→conducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \n   681→the process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \n   682→listed below: \n   683→DESCRIPTION I PURITY LEVEL I \n   684→Significant reduction \n   685→in board discoloration 10,000 ppm \n   686→Reduction in solder 200 ppm \n   687→balling (RMA) \n   688→Reflow of low-residue \n   689→solder paste < 100 ppm \n   690→The paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \n   691→show the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \n   692→ing flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \n   693→cleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \n   694→ture. It is recommended reading if your process may \n   695→require the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \n   696→nitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \n   697→using nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \n   698→mine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \n   699→your process. \n   700→Section I - Technology 11 \n   701→--- PAGE 14 ---\n   702→1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \n   703→WHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \n   704→When selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \n   705→into consideration all aspects that may affect the process \n   706→and production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \n   707→one issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \n   708→lead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \n   709→steps in the selection process are described in the follow- \n   710→ing paragraphs. \n   711→DEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \n   712→The first step is to define the acceptable process profile \n   713→band width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \n   714→accurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \n   715→is too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \n   716→negatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \n   717→show up in the process. The band width should be \n   718→defined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \n   719→profile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \n   720→detailed description. \n   721→DEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \n   722→The next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \n   723→mities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \n   724→mities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \n   725→and oven repeatability under production loading condi- \n   726→tions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \n   727→product on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \n   728→uniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \n   729→defined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \n   730→detailed description. \n   731→SIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \n   732→The oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \n   733→the pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \n   734→below: \n   735→LINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n   736→(MINIMUM) - LOAD FACTOR \n   737→Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   738→speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \n   739→onload area of the reflow oven. \n   740→Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one \n   741→issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \n   742→not lead you to the optimum decision. In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \n   743→speed defined below must be greater than the minimum \n   744→calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \n   745→will not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \n   746→should be determined through testing or by the following \n   747→equation: \n   748→OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH PROCESS - \n   749→SPEED - PROCESS DWELL TIME \n   750→DEFINE PROCESS GAS \n   751→If a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \n   752→no-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \n   753→the purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \n   754→how the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \n   755→at the gas inlet. \n   756→REVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \n   757→The conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \n   758→looked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \n   759→define whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \n   760→required. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \n   761→processing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \n   762→most important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \n   763→for the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \n   764→product. \n   765→RELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \n   766→Once a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \n   767→remains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \n   768→structural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \n   769→designed to withstand the rigors of production? Review \n   770→the product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \n   771→Find out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \n   772→training available? These are the questions that must be \n   773→asked during the selection process. \n   774→INTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \n   775→Conveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \n   776→be an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \n   777→conveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \n   778→and-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \n   779→machines to a host and product flow control schemes if \n   780→needed. \n   781→12 Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n   782→--- PAGE 15 ---\n   783→HOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \n   784→DESCRIPTION The selection steps listed on the previous page should \n   785→all be considered when comparing equipment. When \n   786→multiple ovens pass the selection criteria listed, then a \n   787→proper cost evaluation may be used to compare ovens. \n   788→This is especially important when comparing ovens that \n   789→utilize different heating technologies, as first cost and \n   790→operating costs can be significantly different. This \n   791→section describes various costs that may be included in \n   792→the analysis. \n   793→HOT GAS IR EQUIPMENT COST \n   794→Often, the initial cost of the equipment is the only cost \n   795→considered when comparing equipment. It is certainly \n   796→important, although it is only a part of the total cost \n   797→picture. To make a fair comparison, all costs should be \n   798→analyzed with a common basis, such as annual cost. As \n   799→an example let’s look at amachine that costs $50,000 and \n   800→has an expected life of 5 years, with an interest rate of \n   801→10%. Assume the value of the equipment is zero at the \n   802→end of five years. The annualized cost of the equipment \n   803→is equal to the initial cost times the Capital Recover \n   804→Factor. (3 For an interest rate of 10% with a period of \n   805→5 years, the CRF value is equal to 0.2638. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \n   877→CONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \n   878→Convection oven thermal control requires tight control \n   879→of two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \n   880→maintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \n   881→that both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \n   882→MICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \n   883→be used as an example. The temperature and flow control \n   884→are defined specific to this system. \n   885→CONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \n   886→Temperature control of the system must consider both \n   887→the gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \n   888→ber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \n   889→walls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \n   890→total heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \n   891→the heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \n   892→The reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n   893→(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \n   894→controlled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \n   895→heating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \n   896→enters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \n   897→series of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \n   898→chromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \n   899→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM \n   900→AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n   901→II 4 Y \n   902→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \n   903→can occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \n   904→enters the process chamber through an array of holes. \n   905→The product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \n   906→and the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \n   907→couple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \n   908→repeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \n   909→tive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \n   910→constant conditions. \n   911→The flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \n   912→ducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \n   913→is pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \n   914→flow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \n   915→blower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \n   916→temperature control is accomplished in the same way as \n   917→the dryout and reflow zone. \n   918→The flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \n   919→through the walls in an array of holes to break up the \n   920→boundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \n   921→arrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \n   922→transfer. \n   923→Convection oven thermal control requires tight \n   924→control of two variables; temperature andflow. \n   925→In order to maintain constant heat flux to the \n   926→product, it is critical that both these variables \n   927→are controlled. \n   928→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 15 \n   929→--- PAGE 18 ---\n   930→CONVECTION OVEN WITH \n   931→Convection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \n   932→several reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \n   933→required to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \n   934→extremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \n   935→mize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \n   936→the degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \n   937→a given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \n   938→also required, which can result in a greater opportunity \n   939→for oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \n   940→heater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \n   941→others. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \n   942→job of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \n   943→However, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \n   944→rates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \n   945→CHAMBER DESCRIPTION \n   946→Many of the same principles apply for inerting when \n   947→comparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \n   948→FLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \n   949→similar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \n   950→brought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n   951→etary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \n   952→entry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \n   953→levels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \n   954→controlled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \n   955→in the system and to remove process volatiles. \n   956→In order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \n   957→where the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \n   958→pure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \n   959→zone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \n   960→experiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \n   961→cal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \n   962→from these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \n   963→Here, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \n   964→gas is not detrimental to the process. \n   965→Purity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \n   966→maintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \n   967→flow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \n   968→per hour). \n   969→GAS FLOW \n   970→INPUT FROM \n   971→N2 OR AIR \n   972→SOURCE \n   973→GAS FLOW \n   974→INPUT FROM \n   975→N2 OR AIR \n   976→SOURCE \n   977→16 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n   978→--- PAGE 19 ---\n   979→IR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \n   980→One of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \n   981→repeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \n   982→influenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \n   983→variable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \n   984→pensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \n   985→of repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \n   986→nents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \n   987→LAMP DEFINITION \n   988→The closed loop heat flux control method used in a \n   989→Research, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \n   990→example of one type of control. The primary heat source \n   991→in this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \n   992→lamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \n   993→quartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \n   994→to keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \n   995→lamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \n   996→capacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \n   997→exceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n   998→20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \n   999→the heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \n  1000→heating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \n  1001→is in the medium to long wavelength range. \n  1002→The greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \n  1003→response capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \n  1004→change occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \n  1005→librium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \n  1006→control, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \n  1007→instantly after its removal. \n  1008→CONTROL DEFINITION \n  1009→In order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \n  1010→heat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \n  1011→heat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \n  1012→wall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \n  1013→Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \n  1014→occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \n  1015→equilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \n  1016→closed loop control, as energy can be replaced \n  1017→in the heating chamber instantly after its \n  1018→removal. RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \n  1019→THERMOCOUPLE \n  1020→PRODUCT ”‘7 \\ REFLF JON. $ RE-EMITTED IR \n  1021→VECTION 1 FLOW -- \\P \n  1022→PRODUCT \n  1023→Accurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \n  1024→of the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \n  1025→thermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \n  1026→regulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \n  1027→and convective components are collectively monitored \n  1028→by the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \n  1029→mocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \n  1030→percent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \n  1031→temperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \n  1032→equation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \n  1033→then the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \n  1034→chamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \n  1035→intensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \n  1036→operate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \n  1037→power is modified only enough to compensate for the \n  1038→bulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \n  1039→total heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \n  1040→compensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \n  1041→temperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \n  1042→repeatability off 2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \n  1043→is within this defined band. \n  1044→The ability to operate within this wide band has two \n  1045→significant advantages. The first is that the oven may \n  1046→be loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \n  1047→that profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \n  1048→Loading may begin when all oven temperatures are \n  1049→within f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \n  1050→achieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \n  1051→window was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \n  1052→operation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \n  1053→Profile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \n  1054→increases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \n  1055→decreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \n  1056→from a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \n  1057→changeover time is under 15 minutes. \n  1058→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 17 \n  1059→--- PAGE 20 ---\n  1060→IR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1061→PREHEAT TOP (IR1) Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \n  1062→accomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \n  1063→curs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \n  1064→the heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \n  1065→the benefits of convection, which includes improving \n  1066→populated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \n  1067→amount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \n  1068→required to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \n  1069→ment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \n  1070→convection oven. \n  1071→REFLOW \n  1072→TOP (IR5) DRY TOP (IR3) PREFLOW TOP (IR4) NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1073→The 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \n  1074→dominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \n  1075→environment at low flow levels. The system uses a \n  1076→proprietary gas flow distribution method through the \n  1077→chamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \n  1078→process nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \n  1079→chamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \n  1080→keep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \n  1081→sections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \n  1082→chamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \n  1083→oxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \n  1084→venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \n  1085→system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1086→PREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \n  1087→used once and then expended. This has the advantage \n  1088→that process contaminants are not recirculated which \n  1089→helps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \n  1090→chamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \n  1091→elevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \n  1092→crank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \n  1093→coming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \n  1094→Flow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n  1095→(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \n  1096→field are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n  1097→(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \n  1098→hour (37 cubic meters per hour). These purity levels are \n  1099→measured with the machine fully loaded with product. \n  1100→REFLOW \n  1101→BOTTOM (1R6) DRY BOTTOM (IR3) PREFLOW BOTTOM (IR4) ~~~ ~ \n  1102→The amount of flow in the oven is more \n  1103→related to the quantity required to accom- \n  1104→plish a high purity nitrogen environment, \n  1105→which is considerably less than in the case \n  1106→of a convection oven. \n  1107→NOTE: VENTURI EXHAUSTS ARE ACTIVATED BY COMPRESSED AIR, \n  1108→ALL OTHERS MAY BE NITROGEN OR COMPRESSED AIR. \n  1109→18 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1110→--- PAGE 21 ---\n  1111→EDGE CONVEYOR \n  1112→Proper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \n  1113→as typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \n  1114→the bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \n  1115→has lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \n  1116→product will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \n  1117→straightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \n  1118→As the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \n  1119→period, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \n  1120→system from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1121→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n  1122→(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \n  1123→of the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \n  1124→that runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n  1125→1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \n  1126→of welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \n  1127→structural integrity. \n  1128→The use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \n  1129→rails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \n  1130→minimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \n  1131→As the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \n  1132→chine warm-up period, they will grow and \n  1133→tend to warp. The patented system from \n  1134→Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1135→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. - TENSION STRAIGHTENS RAIL \n  1136→SPRING MAINTAINS TENSION \n  1137→REFERENCES \n  1138→1. “The Transformation of American Industry”, Productivity-Quality Systems, Inc.
