Inc., 1992 1 \n--- PAGE 4 ---\nIN I - TECHNOLOGY ~ \nHOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \nThe process of producing high quality solder joints is \nsimilar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \nIf you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \nimpossible. However, once you understand and imple- \nment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \nSMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \nquality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \nexcellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \nbelow: \nSURFACES MUST BE \nCLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \nII MUST BE \n11 SMALL \nCONTROL VARIABLES \nIn order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \nvariables must be in control. The five general process \nvariables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \nrials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \nment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \na very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \nLet’s take a closer look at each. \nMATERIALS \nThe materials must meet functional specifications so \nthat the product will perform to specification. Of equal \nimportance is the solderability of the assembly. The \nsolder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \nbase materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \npaste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \nsolderability. MACHINES \nThe machines used in the process must be able to perform \nto their specifications. The machines should operate \nwithin the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \nmachine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \na problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \nable. \nMANPOWER \nThe manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \nthe line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \nnance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \noperating it. \nMETHODS \nThe methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \nadhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \nto ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \nshelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \nincluding all data that pertains to it. \nENVIRONMENT \nFinally, the processing environment must be controlled. \nThis includes both the environment inside and outside of \nthe machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \ning area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \nabsorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \nIf the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \nthermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \nagain may cause a defect. \nThe purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \nquality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \nProducing high quality solder joints \nrequires that five general process \nvariables need to be in control; the \nmaterials, machines, manpower, \nmethods, and the environment. \n2 Section I - Technology \n--- PAGE 5 ---\nWHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \nI I I I I I 1; I :I I I I I In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \nwhat is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \na viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \nto a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \nalso take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \ncertain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \ncertain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \nmost astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \nby an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \nof vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \nfive years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \nsoldering defect results on all three types of soldering \nequipment, the following comment was made: “If you \ncould hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \nwater, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \nsolder joints.” Although the above comment was made \nin jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \nthere is considerable truth to what was said. I \nREFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \nHigh quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \nthe optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \npaste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \nevery board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \nsoldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \nmatter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \ndoes matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \nin a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \nrates must be compatible with the solder paste and \ncomponents. The amount of time that the assembly is \nexposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \nmaintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \nmust first be defined and then maintained. 280- \n260- \n240- \nT 220- \nHigh quality, low defect soldering \nrequires identifying the optimum \ntemperature profile for reflowing \nthe solder paste, and repeating it \nover and over. I WETTING ; ‘ TIME , \n30-60 .’ : SEC ~ \nTYPICAL PROFILE \nA typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \nsection (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \nthe preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \nassembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \nis to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \nThe main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \ncomponents of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \ncapacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \nfast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \nThe second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \nsoak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \nthe solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \ntemperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \nzone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \nplished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \nelevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \nof the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \ntemperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \nceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \nThe amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \npoint is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \ntime is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \nwetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \nthe joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \nmary, define the profile, and hit it. \nSection I - Technology 3 \n--- PAGE 6 ---\nHOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \nThis question frequently arrives and can be a source of \nconfusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \ntwenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \nin practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \nflexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \nmakes oven set-up more difficult. \nLet’s review this question from the standpoint of how \nmany zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \ntypically requires two or three different heating rates to \naccomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \nthree “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \nand no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \ngeneric, and one should consult with their solder paste \nvendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \nin the figure could be successfully reflowed using \nthree zones. reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \nheaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \naccomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \nwill not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \nproducts are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \nheating control does help in machines with dual convey- \nors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \nProduct warpage may also be minimized through proper \ncontrol. The bottom line is that most machines have this \nfeature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \nset at the same value. \nEdge heating zones become significant when edge con- \nveyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \nedge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \none back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \nveyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \nunnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \nA second issue is the question of independent top and \nbottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n200 c \nI PREHEAT1 I PREHEAT2 I REFLOW I \nI \nI \nI \nI \nI \nI \nI \nI \nI \nI \nCOOLING I \nTIME \n4 Section I - Technology \n--- PAGE 7 ---\nWHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \nIn reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \nprocess window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \nrange of temperature deviation that can occur during \nreflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \nby the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \nexample, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \nThe actual size of the band will vary depending upon \nsolder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \nSOLDER SPATTER \nAND SOLDER BALLS \nCAUSED BY INSUF- \nFICIENT DRYING. BURNT BOARDS, \nDEWET SOLDER fi COLD SOLDER \nJOINTS, DULL \nSOLDER JOINTS \n(TOO COLD) \nREFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \nIf the product temperature profile is not maintained \nwithin the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \nmay crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \nsive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \nis excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \nsolder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \nvolatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \ncontinues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \nsolder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \nidly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \npreheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \nin the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \ncritical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \nrates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \nnon-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \nExcessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \nboards, damaged components, or possibly solder \ndewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \nbut it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \nDEFINE THE BAND \nThe profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \nassembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \nnot only the solder paste requirements, but also any \nspecific requirements of components or the circuit board \nmaterial. The process band width is defined as the total \ndeviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \nresults. \nOVEN PERFORMANCE \nFrom a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \nto produce consistent results within the reflow profile \nband. The user should be aware of all variables that \ninfluence the band. These variables may be defined as \nproduct related and oven related. By far the most critical \nproduct related non-uniformity is that created by large \nmass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \nan area that has no components as compared with an area \nwith large components, temperature differentials will \nexist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \nsuch as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \ncenter and front-to-back product heating uniformities \nmust also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \ndefect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \nties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \nIn reality the profile is not a line, but rather \na range of temperatures all solder joints \nmust be exposed. \nThe total temperature deviation from \ncomponent thermal mismatch, oven load- \ning, and oven uniformity must be within \nthe band. \nSection I - Technology 5 \n--- PAGE 8 ---\nWHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \nIn order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \nthe defined process band, one needs to better understand \nthe causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \ncauses of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \ncess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \nand heater edge effects, and product loading. \nMASS DIFFERENTIAL \nThe amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \nthrough a heating zone depends upon a number of \nvariables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \nheat is determined by the following equation: \nAT = Q x AX t / (M x Cp) \nWHERE: \nAT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \nQ = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \nA = Exposed Area (cm2) \nt = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \nM = Mass of Object (kg) \nCp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \nThe mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \nto as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \nheat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \nshown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \nconsiderably more thermal mass per unit area than the \narea with the discrete components. This area will be more \ndifficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \nmass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \nlead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \nprofile. \nHIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL J \nmmmm \nmmmm \nmmmm \nmmmm \nmmmm \nmmmm OVEN UNIFORMITY \nA second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \nthe oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \neffects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \nsinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \ncaused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \nmachine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \nheaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \neffect, similar to the one described above. The oven \nuniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \nboard over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \nmatrix should include points on the board edges (front, \nback, left, and right), and the center of the board. \nOVEN REPEATABILITY \nRepeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \ngiven profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \nloading. The figure below shows a typical product \nloading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \nthe figure. \nPRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN b \nL \nLOADING FACTOR = L+s \nThe higher the value of the loading factor, the more \ndifficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \nOvens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \nimportant consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \nuniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \nrunning a profile on a test board, then load the oven \ndown. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \nload and compare with the unloaded profile. \nThe three main causes of non-uniform heating in \nthe SMT reflow process include mass differential \non the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \nand product loading. \n6 Section I - Technology \n--- PAGE 9 ---\nWHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \nThere are three different heating modes involved wit \nmost SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \nand infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \nmodes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \neasiest way to understand each of these heating mode \nis through example. \nCONVECTION \nConvection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \nair, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \nSMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \nprovides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \nprovides convective heating. Convection heating c \ncooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \nOnly the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \nis actually transferring heat. \n2ONVECTION I - \nHOT . \nAIR \\ \nFLOW - COLD OBJECT IS - HEATED BY CONVECTION \nFROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \nCONDUCTION \nConduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \nof different temperature are in contact with each other \nA good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \nburner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \ncontact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \noccurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \ntial exists within the mass. \nReferring to a system as 100% convection or \n100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \nby convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \nalways present in signijicant quantities. CONDUCTION \nHEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \nEQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \nRADIATION \nInfrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \nferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \nexample of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \nis a non-contact heat transfer. \nRADIATION I \nIn SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \nphase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \nthe heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \nconvection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \ndominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \nfer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \nin the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \ndominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \nproduct during the heating process. Heat is transferred \nthrough the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \nSection I - Technology 7 \n--- PAGE 10 ---\nHOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n95.38 Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \ndifferent temperatures are in sight of each other. The \nheat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \nof infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \nthe wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \nthe absolute temperature of the object. As the source \ntemperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \nexponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \ntemperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \nthe source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \nSMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \nas not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \nthese low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \nwavelength ranges. \n111111lI INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \nIn order to understand what parameters are important in \ninfrared heating, one can consider the general equation \nfor heat transfer between the heat source and the object \nbeing heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \nequation, but rather to point out the significance of what \nis involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \nfollows: \nQ = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \nWHERE: \nQ = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \nV = Geometric view factor (0-1) \nEs = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \nAt = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \nK = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \nTs = Source temperature (OK) \nTt = Target temperature (OK) (5.67 x w/cm2/”K4) \nAbsorption characteristics of solder \npaste and circuit board material makes \nIR an efficient means of heating. The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \nthat leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \nthe oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \nin the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \nview factor comes in product design. If two very large \ncomponents are in close proximity to each other, the \nview factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \nwhich makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \nf IRSOURCE \nTHIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \nADJACENT COMPONENTS \nRESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \nFACTOR \nThe source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \nare in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \ntions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \nenergy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \nas they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \nboard material, solder paste, and the components all \nabsorb quite well. The figure below shows the spectral \nabsorptivity for an RMA solder paste.(3) \nI \nloooo t \n! 97.39 I4 I \n96’70 96.04 I! \nControl of infrared is generally done by controlling the \nsource temperature. Providing that the overall source \nemission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \nrepeatability. \n8 Section I - Technology \n--- PAGE 11 ---\nHOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \nConvection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \ntemperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \nture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \nheated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \nbe cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \ntion is to look at the convection equation and note what \nis important and what is not important. \nCONVECTION EQUATION \nThe following is the general equation for convection \nheating or cooling: \nQ=HxAT \nWHERE: \nQ = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \nH = Convective film coefficient \nAT = Temperature difference between (W/cm2-”C) \nfluid and object (“C) \nThe amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \neither changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \ntemperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \ntemperature will increase the temperature difference, \nand thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \nproportionally. \nThe convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \nimpact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \nrelated to a number of variables, including velocity of \nthe flow and angle of attack of the flow. \nIncreasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \nlow velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \nnice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n~ ~~ ~~ \nPerpendicular flow convection breaks \nup surface thermal boundary layers, \nwhich enhances heat transfer. very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \na higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \nmore effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \ncations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \nthe amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \ntical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \ncomponents to shift around. The flow direction also has \na significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \nis shown below: \nPARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \nI FLOW DIRECTION 4 - \nBOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \nCAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \nBOARD, WHICH REDUCES \nHEAT TRANSFER \nNote that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \nreferred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \nlayer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \nheat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \nthe boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \nfreely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \nperpendicular flow convection: \nPERPENDICULAR FLOW \nFLOW DIRECTION \nttt \nNO BOUNDARY \nLAYER \nNO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \nENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \nTRANSFER \nIn the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \nthere is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \nperpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \ncooling results. \nSection I - Technology 9 \n--- PAGE 12 ---\nWHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \nIn SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \npure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \nover the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \nin not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \nwould need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \nthe case. However, some systems are more dominated by \nconvection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \nsource. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \nconvection dominated machine as a convection ma- \nchine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \nCONVECTION \nBoth convection and IR have their unique advantages \nand limitations. In convective systems, typically the \ntemperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \nture of the product in a given zone. This has the \nadvantage in that the product temperature will never \nexceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \nheating a product that has a large difference in the mass \nof the components and distribution. In this case, the \nlarger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \ncomponents will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \nis not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \nheat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \nmay be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \nefficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \nCONVECTION \nLess sensitive to shading \nHeats more uniformly \nAT (Source-target) is small \nLow thermal mass of air requires \nhigh flow rates to transfer heat \nInefficient (20 to 30%) \nWill heat leads first, increasing \nchance of solder wicking. is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \nloop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \nreheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \nlimitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \nlevels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \nthat if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \nsoldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \nproduct becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \nucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \nas typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \nIR \nIR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \nefficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \nsolder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \nhave the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \nneeded. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \ndense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \nlimitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \nbetween the source and the product is required to transfer \nthe heat. Products with high component mass differen- \ntial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \nto accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \nreflowing products with component sizes up to and \nincluding 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \narrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \nsystem. \nIR \nControl source temperature only \nHigh efficiency (60-70%) \nEasy to control rate of rise \nMore sensitive to shading \nLess uniform high mass differences \nAT (Source-target) is large \n10 Section I - Technology \n--- PAGE 13 ---\nWHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n~ \nEase of cleaning flux \nresidues (RMA) Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \na significant process consideration. The issue is really \nnot the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \nto reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \nthe presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \ngenerally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \nsoldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \nfluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \nfuture trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \npaste. The future trends include two major process \nchanges that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \natmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n500 ppm FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \nFine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \nto obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \nsizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \nparticles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \nvolume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \nRATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \nRATIO = 6 / D \nWHERE: \nD = Particle Diameter \nFOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \nGREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \nWITH LARGER PARTICLES. \nThus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \nWith more available surface area in the solder paste, \nthere is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \nmind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \nthe temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \nand the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \nthat one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \nThis can only be done through proper oven design. NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \nThe second technology change that is driving the need \ntowards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \nclean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \nis creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \nrequires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \nnents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \nprocess must not add any significant oxidation to the \nprocess. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \naggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \ncleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \nproduct. These solder balls eventually can create short \ncircuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \nin a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \nbility of creating solder balls. \nA good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \nment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \nNational Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \nconducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \nthe process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \nlisted below: \nDESCRIPTION I PURITY LEVEL I \nSignificant reduction \nin board discoloration 10,000 ppm \nReduction in solder 200 ppm \nballing (RMA) \nReflow of low-residue \nsolder paste < 100 ppm \nThe paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \nshow the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \ning flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \ncleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \nture. It is recommended reading if your process may \nrequire the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \nnitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \nusing nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \nmine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \nyour process. \nSection I - Technology 11 \n--- PAGE 14 ---\n1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \nWHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \nWhen selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \ninto consideration all aspects that may affect the process \nand production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \none issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \nlead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \nsteps in the selection process are described in the follow- \ning paragraphs. \nDEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \nThe first step is to define the acceptable process profile \nband width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \naccurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \nis too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \nnegatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \nshow up in the process. The band width should be \ndefined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \nprofile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \ndetailed description. \nDEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \nThe next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \nmities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \nmities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \nand oven repeatability under production loading condi- \ntions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \nproduct on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \nuniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \ndefined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \ndetailed description. \nSIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \nThe oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \nthe pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \nbelow: \nLINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n(MINIMUM) - LOAD FACTOR \nOperating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \nspeed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \nonload area of the reflow oven. \nTry to avoid becoming too focussed on one \nissue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \nnot lead you to the optimum decision. In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \nspeed defined below must be greater than the minimum \ncalculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \nwill not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \nshould be determined through testing or by the following \nequation: \nOVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH PROCESS - \nSPEED - PROCESS DWELL TIME \nDEFINE PROCESS GAS \nIf a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \nno-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \nthe purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \nhow the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \nat the gas inlet. \nREVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \nThe conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \nlooked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \ndefine whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \nrequired. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \nprocessing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \nmost important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \nfor the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \nproduct. \nRELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \nOnce a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \nremains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \nstructural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \ndesigned to withstand the rigors of production? Review \nthe product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \nFind out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \ntraining available? These are the questions that must be \nasked during the selection process. \nINTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \nConveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \nbe an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \nconveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \nand-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \nmachines to a host and product flow control schemes if \nneeded. \n12 Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n--- PAGE 15 ---\nHOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \nDESCRIPTION The selection steps listed on the previous page should \nall be considered when comparing equipment. When \nmultiple ovens pass the selection criteria listed, then a \nproper cost evaluation may be used to compare ovens. \nThis is especially important when comparing ovens that \nutilize different heating technologies, as first cost and \noperating costs can be significantly different. This \nsection describes various costs that may be included in \nthe analysis. \nHOT GAS IR EQUIPMENT COST \nOften, the initial cost of the equipment is the only cost \nconsidered when comparing equipment. It is certainly \nimportant, although it is only a part of the total cost \npicture. To make a fair comparison, all costs should be \nanalyzed with a common basis, such as annual cost. As \nan example let’s look at amachine that costs $50,000 and \nhas an expected life of 5 years, with an interest rate of \n10%. Assume the value of the equipment is zero at the \nend of five years. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \nCONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \nConvection oven thermal control requires tight control \nof two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \nmaintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \nthat both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \nMICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \nbe used as an example. The temperature and flow control \nare defined specific to this system. \nCONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \nTemperature control of the system must consider both \nthe gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \nber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \nwalls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \ntotal heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \nthe heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \nThe reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \ncontrolled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \nheating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \nenters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \nseries of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \nchromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \nGAS FLOW INPUT FROM \nAIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \nII 4 Y \nGAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \ncan occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \nenters the process chamber through an array of holes. \nThe product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \nand the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \ncouple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \nrepeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \ntive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \nconstant conditions. \nThe flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \nducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \nis pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \nflow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \nblower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \ntemperature control is accomplished in the same way as \nthe dryout and reflow zone. \nThe flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \nthrough the walls in an array of holes to break up the \nboundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \narrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \ntransfer. \nConvection oven thermal control requires tight \ncontrol of two variables; temperature andflow. \nIn order to maintain constant heat flux to the \nproduct, it is critical that both these variables \nare controlled. \nSection I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 15 \n--- PAGE 18 ---\nCONVECTION OVEN WITH \nConvection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \nseveral reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \nrequired to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \nextremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \nmize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \nthe degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \na given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \nalso required, which can result in a greater opportunity \nfor oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \nheater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \nothers. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \njob of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \nHowever, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \nrates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \nCHAMBER DESCRIPTION \nMany of the same principles apply for inerting when \ncomparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \nFLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \nsimilar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \nbrought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \netary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \nentry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \nlevels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \ncontrolled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \nin the system and to remove process volatiles. \nIn order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \nwhere the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \npure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \nzone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \nexperiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \ncal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \nfrom these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \nHere, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \ngas is not detrimental to the process. \nPurity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \nmaintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \nflow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \nper hour). \nGAS FLOW \nINPUT FROM \nN2 OR AIR \nSOURCE \nGAS FLOW \nINPUT FROM \nN2 OR AIR \nSOURCE \n16 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n--- PAGE 19 ---\nIR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \nOne of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \nrepeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \ninfluenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \nvariable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \npensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \nof repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \nnents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \nLAMP DEFINITION \nThe closed loop heat flux control method used in a \nResearch, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \nexample of one type of control. The primary heat source \nin this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \nlamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \nquartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \nto keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \nlamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \ncapacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \nexceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \nthe heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \nheating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \nis in the medium to long wavelength range. \nThe greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \nresponse capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \nchange occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \nlibrium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \ncontrol, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \ninstantly after its removal. \nCONTROL DEFINITION \nIn order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \nheat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \nheat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \nwall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \nWithin 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \noccurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \nequilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \nclosed loop control, as energy can be replaced \nin the heating chamber instantly after its \nremoval. RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \nTHERMOCOUPLE \nPRODUCT ”‘7 \\ REFLF JON. $ RE-EMITTED IR \nVECTION 1 FLOW -- \\P \nPRODUCT \nAccurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \nof the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \nthermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \nregulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \nand convective components are collectively monitored \nby the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \nmocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \npercent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \ntemperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \nequation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \nthen the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \nchamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \nintensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \noperate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \npower is modified only enough to compensate for the \nbulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \ntotal heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \ncompensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \ntemperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \nrepeatability off 2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \nis within this defined band. \nThe ability to operate within this wide band has two \nsignificant advantages. The first is that the oven may \nbe loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \nthat profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \nLoading may begin when all oven temperatures are \nwithin f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \nachieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \nwindow was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \noperation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \nProfile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \nincreases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \ndecreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \nfrom a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \nchangeover time is under 15 minutes. \nSection I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 17 \n--- PAGE 20 ---\nIR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \nPREHEAT TOP (IR1) Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \naccomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \ncurs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \nthe heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \nthe benefits of convection, which includes improving \npopulated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \namount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \nrequired to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \nment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \nconvection oven. \nREFLOW \nTOP (IR5) DRY TOP (IR3) PREFLOW TOP (IR4) NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \nThe 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \ndominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \nenvironment at low flow levels. The system uses a \nproprietary gas flow distribution method through the \nchamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \nprocess nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \nchamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \nkeep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \nsections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \nchamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \noxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \nventuri exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \nsystem and to remove process volatiles. \nPREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \nused once and then expended. This has the advantage \nthat process contaminants are not recirculated which \nhelps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \nchamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \nelevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \ncrank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \ncoming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \nFlow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \nfield are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \nhour (37 cubic meters per hour). These purity levels are \nmeasured with the machine fully loaded with product. \nREFLOW \nBOTTOM (1R6) DRY BOTTOM (IR3) PREFLOW BOTTOM (IR4) ~~~ ~ \nThe amount of flow in the oven is more \nrelated to the quantity required to accom- \nplish a high purity nitrogen environment, \nwhich is considerably less than in the case \nof a convection oven. \nNOTE: VENTURI EXHAUSTS ARE ACTIVATED BY COMPRESSED AIR, \nALL OTHERS MAY BE NITROGEN OR COMPRESSED AIR. \n18 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n--- PAGE 21 ---\nEDGE CONVEYOR \nProper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \nas typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \nthe bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \nhas lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \nproduct will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \nstraightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \nAs the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \nperiod, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \nsystem from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \nrails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \nof the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \nthat runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \nof welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \nstructural integrity. \nThe use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \nrails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \nminimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \nAs the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \nchine warm-up period, they will grow and \ntend to warp. Temp1) / (Time2 - Time1)\ntemps = preheat_data['temp_c'].values\ntimes = preheat_data['time_s'].values\n\nif len(temps) > 1:\n    ramp_rates = np.diff(temps) / np.diff(times)\n    max_ramp = np.max(ramp_rates)\n```\n\n### Time Above Liquidus (TAL) Calculation\nCalculate how long temperature exceeds liquidus temperature:\n\n```python\n# Liquidus temperature from solder type\nliquidus_c = 183  # For Sn63Pb37\n\n# Filter data above liquidus\nabove_liquidus = df_tc[df_tc['temp_c'] >= liquidus_c]\n\n# Calculate duration\nif len(above_liquidus) > 1:\n    tal_s = above_liquidus['time_s'].max() - above_liquidus['time_s'].min()\n```\n\n### Peak Temperature Detection\nIdentify the maximum temperature reached during reflow:\n\n```python\npeak_temp = df_tc['temp_c'].max()\npeak_time = df_tc.loc[df_tc['temp_c'].idxmax(), 'time_s']\n```\n\n## Common Patterns\n\n### Group by Multiple Columns\n```python\n# Group thermocouple data by run and thermocouple ID\nfor (run_id, tc_id), group in df.groupby(['run_id', 'tc_id']):\n    # Process each thermocouple per run\n    max_temp = group['temp_c'].max()\n```\n\n### Merge with Reference Data\n```python\n# Merge thermocouple data with MES log for solder liquidus info\ndf_merged = df_thermocouples.merge(\n    df_mes[['run_id', 'solder_liquidus_c', 'board_family']],\n    on='run_id'\n)\n```\n\n### Filter by Zone ID\n```python\n# Zone ID identifies different heating zones in reflow oven\n# Zone 1: Preheat\n# Zone 2-3: Dryout/Soak\n# Zone 4+: Reflow\n\ndf_preheat = df[df['zone_id'] == 1]\ndf_reflow = df[df['zone_id'] >= 4]\n```\n\n## Common Pitfalls\n- Ensure time values are sorted before calculating ramp rates\n- Use `groupby().apply()` for complex multi-column calculations\n- Remember to round float values to 2 decimals for JSON output\n- Handle missing data (NaN) appropriately - Proper preheat ramp rates\n  2. Adequate TAL window\n  3. Minimum peak temperature\n\n## Loading Factor\n- **Definition**: L / (L + S) where L = product length, S = gap to next product\n- **Maximum Typical Value**: 0.5 to 0.9 depending on machine\n- **Impact**: Affects oven repeatability under production load\n\n## Thermocouple Selection\n- **Most Representative Location**: Largest mass component (thermal reference)\n- **Zone Measurement**: Zone ID indicates which heating zone reading was taken\n- **Data Quality**: Use highest quality thermocouple reading (typically largest mass point) for process compliance\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":3,"cache_creation_input_tokens":960,"cache_read_input_tokens":46854,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":960,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":24},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"b4bc8941-9346-455b-b845-ff29f120c066","uuid":"e1be5817-667b-4f08-9083-5d9dd069d551"}

Always round to 2 decimals\n    \"optional_field\": None  # Use None for null values\n}\n\n# Write formatted JSON\nwith open('/app/output/result.json', 'w') as f:\n    json.dump(output_data, f, indent=2)\n```\n\n## Sorting Conventions\n- Sort arrays by `run_id` (ascending, alphanumeric)\n- Then by `board_family` (alphabetical)\n- Then by `board_id` (alphanumeric)\n\n```python\nimport pandas as pd\n\n# Sort by multiple columns\ndf_sorted = df.sort_values(['run_id', 'board_family', 'board_id'])\n\n# Convert to list of dicts\nresult_list = df_sorted.to_dict('records')\n```\n\n## Common Patterns\n\n### Nested Dictionary Structure\n```python\n{\n    \"metric_limit\": 2.0,\n    \"failing_items\": [\"R0001\", \"R0003\"],\n    \"data_by_run\": {\n        \"R0001\": {\n            \"tc_id\": \"TC1\",\n            \"calculated_value\": 1.23\n        },\n        \"R0002\": {\n            \"tc_id\": \"TC2\",\n            \"calculated_value\": 2.45\n        }\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### List of Objects Structure\n```python\n[\n    {\n        \"run_id\": \"R0001\",\n        \"tc_id\": \"TC1\",\n        \"metric\": 1.23,\n        \"status\": \"compliant\"\n    },\n    {\n        \"run_id\": \"R0002\",\n        \"tc_id\": \"TC2\",\n        \"metric\": 2.45,\n        \"status\": \"non-compliant\"\n    }\n]\n```\n\n## Type Conversions\n```python\n# Ensure proper JSON types\njson_data = {\n    \"integer\": int(value),           # int type\n    \"float\": float(round(value, 2)), # float with 2 decimals\n    \"string\": str(value),             # string type\n    \"boolean\": value > threshold,     # true/false\n    \"null\": None if missing else val  # **Violating Runs:** 4 (R0018, R0035, R0044, R0060)\n- **Thermocouple Used:** Largest mass location (most representative)\n\n**Key Findings:**\n- Most runs comply (56/60)\n- Violations found in preheat (multiple thermocouples per run)\n- **Compliant:** 136 measurements (38.7%)\n- **Non-compliant:** 215 measurements (61.3%)\n\n**Key Findings:**\n- Many runs exceed maximum TAL (main reflow segment > 60s)\n- TAL calculated as longest continuous segment above liquidus\n- Excessive TAL (>60s) risk: intermetallic formation and brittle solder joints\n- Insufficient TAL (<30s) risk: incomplete reflow\n\n#### Q03: Peak Temperature Compliance\n**File:** `/app/output/q03.json`\n\n- **Specification:** Peak ≥ Liquidus + 20°C (handbook page 163)\n- **Runs Analyzed:** 60\n- **Failing Runs:** 13 (R0004, R0005, R0008, R0009, R0011, R0012, R0013, R0015, R0016, R0033, R0037, R0045, R0052)\n- **Pass Rate:** 78.3% (47/60)\n\n**Key Findings:**\n- Thermocouple selected: largest mass location (most representative for reflow)\n- Failures indicate insufficient reflow heat in process zones\n- Peak temperature directly impacts solder joint quality and wetting\n\n#### Q04: Conveyor Speed Feasibility\n**File:** `/app/output/q04.json`\n\n- **Requirement Formula:** MIN_SPEED = (boards_per_min × board_length_cm) / loading_factor\n- **Total Runs:** 60\n- **Feasible:** 51 (85.0%)\n- **Infeasible:** 9 (15.0%)\n\n**Infeasible Runs:** R0002, R0011, R0015, R0019, R0020, R0029, R0040, R0048, R0055\n\n**Key Findings:**\n- Most production speeds are adequate\n- Failures indicate bottleneck risk at oven entrance\n- Lower speed provides longer dwell time for proper thermal profile\n\n#### Q05: Best Run Selection per Board Family\n**File:** `/app/output/q05.json`\n\nSelection Conveyor speed feasibility (must meet requirement)\n2. Yield percentage (higher is better)\n3. Reflow Machine Handbook)\n- **Thermocouple Data:** /app/data/thermocouples.csv (18,835 temperature readings)\n- **MES Log:** /app/data/mes_log.csv (60 production runs)\n- **Test Defects:** /app/data/test_defects.csv (quality yield data)\n\n## Key Specifications from Handbook\n\n### Preheat Zone\n- **Temperature Range:** 100°C - 150°C\n- **Max Ramp Rate:** < 2°C/second\n- **Purpose:** Minimize thermal shock and volatiles release\n\n### Reflow Zone\n- **Peak Temperature:** Liquidus + 20°C minimum\n- **Time Above Liquidus:** 30-60 seconds\n- **Purpose:** Ensure complete wetting and intermetallic formation control\n\n### Conveyor System\n- **Board Contact:** Edge conveyor (0.17 inches / 4mm contact)\n- **Speed Calculation:** (boards_per_min × board_length) / loading_factor\n- **Impact:** Determines dwell time in each heating zone\n\n## Recommendations\n\n1. **Preheat Compliance:** Investigate R0018, R0035, R0044, R0060 for excessive ramp rates\n2. **TAL Control:** Review reflow zone temperatures to reduce excessive TAL (>60s)\n3. **Peak Temperature:** Address 13 failing runs (R0004, R0005, etc.) - increase reflow zone setpoints\n4. **Conveyor Speed:** Optimize 9 runs with insufficient speed (risk of queue backup)\n5. **Best Practices:** Use identified \"best runs\" as reference standards for each board family\n\n## Quality Metrics\n- **Overall Compliance Rate:** 65-80% depending on metric\n- **Critical Failures:** 13 runs (peak temperature), 4 runs (ramp rate)\n- **Efficiency Issues:** 9 runs (conveyor speed)\nEOF\ncat /app/RESULTS_SUMMARY.md\n","description":"Create comprehensive results summary"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":321,"cache_read_input_tokens":74969,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":321,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":7},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"b4bc8941-9346-455b-b845-ff29f120c066","uuid":"50c57e35-a788-4745-86fd-2113836c76b4"}
